|**NPC Nodes**|Individual characters with personal Schemas. Each NPC’s Assessment Card (Document 2B) feeds the node’s data. The NPC’s front potential section on the card lists which fronts this node drives.|
| :- | :- |
|**Institutional Nodes**|Colleges, guilds, governments, military organizations. Each institution has an institutional Schema (Document 1B) and drives institutional fronts. Institutional nodes are larger than NPC nodes — they contain multiple NPCs within them, each of whom may also be an individual node.|
|**Location Nodes**|Reactive environments that function as fronts (Project Slaughterhouse). The Titanwood, the Crash Site, the Pythia Gauntlet. Location nodes connect to institutional and NPC nodes through operational relationships.|
|**Territorial Nodes**|Regions defined by Zoetrope position and isotope profile. Territorial nodes contain institutional, NPC, and location nodes within them. They operate at the longest timescale and connect to other territorial nodes through trade, diplomacy, and conflict.|
|**The Party**|The party is a node. It has connections to NPCs, institutions, and locations. Its edges track the same three properties (trust, debt, tension) as every other connection. The party’s position on the map reveals which parts of the network they can influence and which are beyond their current reach.|

**Node Hierarchy**

Nodes nest. A territorial node contains institutional nodes. An institutional node contains NPC nodes. The map can be drawn at different zoom levels depending on what the GM needs to see:

**Adventure zoom:** Shows only the party, the NPCs they’ve met, and the connections between them. The most common view during play. Fits on one page.

**Campaign zoom:** Shows institutions, their inter-institutional connections, and the key NPCs who drive each institution’s fronts. Used during prep to understand the political landscape.

**Setting zoom:** Shows territories, their inter-territorial connections, and the institutional forces operating within each. Used for long-arc planning and Zoetrope tracking.

The same map at three resolutions. The edges between nodes carry the same three properties at every zoom level. Trust between two NPCs and trust between two nations describe the same thing at different scales.

**EDGES**

Every connection between two nodes is an edge with three properties. The properties are rated on a qualitative scale (Low / Medium / High) rather than numerical values. The qualitative rating is sufficient for the GM to make propagation decisions during play.

**Trust**

How freely information flows between the two nodes. Trust governs the quality of communication — not whether nodes communicate (most do), but how honestly and completely.

|**High Trust**|Honest, complete communication. Each node shares relevant information willingly. Intelligence flows freely in both directions. Information passed along this edge is reliable — the receiving node can act on it without verification.|
| :- | :- |
|**Medium Trust**|Filtered communication. Each node shares some information but withholds what they consider sensitive. Intelligence is partial. Information passed along this edge should be verified before critical decisions.|
|**Low Trust**|Guarded or deceptive communication. Each node assumes the other is withholding or distorting. Intelligence is unreliable. Information passed along this edge may be deliberately misleading — the receiving node must treat it as unverified at best, disinformation at worst.|

Trust determines how QUICKLY and ACCURATELY consequences propagate. When a clock advances on a front, the symptoms travel to connected fronts along edges. High-trust edges transmit accurate symptoms rapidly — the connected front knows what happened and responds appropriately. Low-trust edges transmit distorted symptoms slowly — the connected front learns something happened but misunderstands what, and responds based on the misunderstanding.

Distorted propagation along low-trust edges IS a story generator. Front A’s clock advances. Front B learns about it through a low-trust edge and receives a garbled version. Front B responds to the garbled version, producing consequences that make no sense to Front A. The miscommunication cascades. Neither front understands why the other is behaving strangely. The party, encountering both fronts’ symptoms, sees two sets of anomalous behavior and must investigate to discover that the source is a miscommunication, not a conspiracy.

**Debt**

Latent obligations between two nodes. Who owes whom, and what. Debt is the stored energy in the relationship — it creates leverage and shapes behavior even when not actively invoked.

|**Owes**|This node owes the connected node. The connected node has leverage — the ability to call in the debt at a moment of their choosing. The indebted node’s behavior is partially constrained by the awareness that the debt could be called.|
| :- | :- |
|**Owed**|The connected node owes this one. This node has leverage. The debt is an asset that can be deployed strategically — called in during a crisis, traded to a third party, or held as insurance.|
|**Mutual**|Both nodes owe each other. Neither has clear leverage. The debts may be of different kinds (one owes money, the other owes loyalty) creating a complex interdependency that neither can cleanly resolve.|
|**None**|No outstanding obligations. The cleanest possible relationship state. Also the least interesting — no stored energy means no leverage and no constraint.|

Debt interacts with trust. A high-trust edge with debt is a genuine obligation that both parties acknowledge — the debt will be honored because the relationship supports it. A low-trust edge with debt is a weapon — the debt will be called at the worst possible moment because the debtor does not trust the creditor to be reasonable. Debt on a low-trust edge is a bomb waiting for a trigger.

Unresolved debt accumulates tension (see below). Every session that passes with an outstanding debt increases the tension on the edge by one step. The longer a debt goes uncalled, the more weight it carries when it finally arrives. This is Chekhov’s Gun applied to relationships — every placed debt must eventually resolve, and the delay between placement and resolution increases the dramatic impact.

**Tension**

Current stress on the connection. The pressure that accumulates from unresolved conflicts, competing interests, and outstanding debts. Tension is the measure of how close the relationship is to a state change.

|**Low Tension**|The relationship is stable. No active conflicts. No pressing unresolved issues. The edge is quiet. Consequences propagate along it without producing secondary effects.|
| :- | :- |
|**Medium Tension**|Active stress. Competing interests, unresolved disagreements, or accumulated small frictions. The edge is loaded. Consequences propagating along it may amplify — a small clock advancement on one front could trigger a disproportionate response from the connected front because the tension converts neutral information into threatening information.|
|**High Tension**|The relationship is approaching a state change. One more significant event will push it past the threshold. The edge is a fault line. Any consequence propagating along it will be amplified dramatically — even minor events become crises because the relationship has no remaining capacity to absorb stress.|

**STATE CHANGES**

Every edge has a state: Ally, Neutral, Rival, or Enemy. The state describes the overall character of the relationship. When tension exceeds its threshold, the state changes.

**The State Spectrum**

|**State**|**Character**|**Mechanical Effect**|
| :- | :- | :- |
|**Ally**|Active cooperation. The nodes coordinate actions, share resources, and support each other’s fronts. Each node’s clock advancements may benefit the other.|Consequences propagate as support. A clock advancement on one front can retard a threatening clock on the ally’s front, or provide resources that accelerate a beneficial clock.|
|**Neutral**|No active cooperation or opposition. The nodes are aware of each other but do not coordinate. Consequences propagate as information, not as support or threat.|Consequences propagate as data. The connected front learns what happened and may or may not respond. The response, if any, is based on self-interest rather than alliance.|
|**Rival**|Active competition. The nodes pursue conflicting goals or compete for the same resources. Each node’s clock advancements may threaten the other.|Consequences propagate as pressure. A clock advancement on one front increases tension on the rival’s connected edges and may tick a defensive or retaliatory clock on the rival’s front.|
|**Enemy**|Active opposition. The nodes are working against each other. Each node’s clock advancements directly threaten the other’s interests.|Consequences propagate as attack. A clock advancement on one front directly ticks an adversarial clock on the enemy’s front. The two fronts are in direct mechanical opposition.|

**Threshold Crossings**

When tension on an edge reaches High and a triggering event occurs, the state changes one step in the direction of the tension’s source:

**Negative tension:** Tension from competing interests or unresolved conflict pushes the state toward hostility: Ally → Neutral → Rival → Enemy.

**Positive tension:** Tension from an act of sacrifice, trust-building, or debt resolution pushes the state toward cooperation: Enemy → Rival → Neutral → Ally.

After a state change, tension resets to Low. The relationship has reorganized into its new state. Tension begins accumulating again from the new baseline.

State changes are dramatic events. They should emerge from play, not be imposed arbitrarily. The GM tracks tension qualitatively through accumulated events. When a specific in-game event pushes the relationship past the threshold, the state change happens in response to that event, producing a visible moment the party (and the affected NPCs) can react to.

**State Changes as Bangs**

Every state change is a bang at Vertebra 4 (Clocks) — a situation that demands an immediate response with no predetermined right answer. When two allied institutions become rivals, every NPC connected to both must choose a side, renegotiate their position, or find a way to maintain both relationships. The state change produces forced-choice situations that cascade through the network. The more edges connected to the changed relationship, the more NPCs and fronts are affected, and the more bangs are generated.

**CONSEQUENCE PROPAGATION**

When a clock advances on a front, the consequences travel along the edges to connected fronts. The propagation follows four rules.

**Rule 1: Consequences Follow Edges**

Consequences do not jump between unconnected nodes. If Front A’s clock advances and Front A has no edge to Front C, then Front C does not feel the consequences directly. Front C may feel them indirectly if Front B is connected to both A and C — the consequences propagate from A to B, then from B to C. Each intermediary edge filters and potentially distorts the consequence.

**Rule 2: Trust Governs Accuracy**

A consequence propagating along a high-trust edge arrives accurately. The receiving front knows what happened and can respond appropriately. A consequence propagating along a low-trust edge arrives distorted. The receiving front may misunderstand the cause, the severity, or the intent behind the event. The distortion IS a story seed — the misunderstanding produces responses that create new complications.

**Rule 3: Tension Governs Amplification**

A consequence propagating along a low-tension edge arrives at its actual magnitude. A consequence propagating along a high-tension edge is AMPLIFIED. The receiving front interprets the event as more threatening, more significant, or more urgent than it actually is because the loaded relationship converts neutral information into alarming information. A routine clock advancement on one front, propagating through a high-tension edge, can trigger a crisis-level response on the connected front — not because the event warranted it but because the relationship’s stored stress amplified it.

**Rule 4: State Governs Direction**

The edge’s state determines WHETHER the consequence helps or hinders the receiving front. An ally edge converts consequences into support. An enemy edge converts consequences into threat. A neutral edge converts consequences into data. A rival edge converts consequences into competitive pressure. The same clock advancement produces four different effects depending on which state the connecting edge occupies.

These four rules combined produce emergent complexity. A single clock advancement propagates through multiple edges, each with different trust, tension, and state values, producing different effects at each receiving front. The network of edges transforms one event into multiple consequences, each filtered and shaped by the connection it travels through. The GM does not need to author each consequence individually. The map’s properties determine them.

**BUILDING AND MAINTAINING THE MAP**

**Initial Construction**

Draw the map during prep, after the fronts are defined (Document 3A). Place every active front as a node. Draw an edge between every pair of fronts that have a relationship — institutional, personal, geographic, or economic. Assign the three properties (trust, debt, tension) and the state (ally/neutral/rival/enemy) to each edge based on the setting’s established situation.

Not every pair of fronts needs an edge. Only fronts that interact, compete, cooperate, or share resources need connections. A Stormpeak front and a Sovereign Exchange front operating in the same territory are connected. A Stormpeak front in the far north and a Sovereign Exchange front on the southern coast probably are not, unless a specific relationship links them.

**Adding Nodes During Play**

When a new NPC becomes notable (triggers an Assessment Card) or a new front emerges from play, add them to the map. Draw edges to existing nodes based on the relationships that have been established. New nodes start with few edges. Over the campaign, they accumulate connections as relationships form.

The party is a node. As they form relationships with NPCs, make agreements with institutions, and establish presence in territories, their edges accumulate. The party’s map of connections IS their political influence. A party with many ally-state edges to powerful nodes has significant structural leverage. A party with few edges is isolated and vulnerable to being acted upon without allies.

**Updating Properties**

After each session, review the map for changes:

**Trust changes:** Did any event increase or decrease trust between two nodes? Successful cooperation increases trust. Betrayal or deception decreases it. Adjust accordingly.

**Debt changes:** Were any debts created, called in, or traded? New favors, unmet obligations, or transfers of leverage change the debt landscape.

**Tension changes:** Did any event increase tension between nodes? Competing over the same resource, conflicting clock objectives, or propagated consequences from other edges all increase tension. Resolution of conflicts or acts of reconciliation decrease tension.

**State changes:** Did any edge’s tension cross the threshold? If yes, process the state change and reset tension to Low.

**Physical Format**

The map is whatever the GM can maintain. Practical options:

|**Index cards on a wall**|One card per node (doubling as the Assessment Card). String or drawn lines between cards for edges. Properties written on the strings or noted on the cards. Maximum visibility. Requires wall space.|
| :- | :- |
|**Digital graph tool**|Any diagramming tool that supports labeled edges. Scalable, searchable, shareable. Requires screen and software comfort.|
|**Notebook sketch**|A hand-drawn diagram in the campaign notebook. Compact and portable. Harder to update cleanly. Best for small networks (under 15 nodes).|
|**Spreadsheet matrix**|Nodes on rows and columns. Each cell contains the edge properties for that pair. Compact, easy to update, but loses the spatial intuition of a visual map.|

The format does not matter. What matters is that the GM can look at the map and immediately answer: “When Front A’s clock advances, which other fronts feel it, through what quality of connection, and how will they respond?” If the format answers that question at a glance, it is sufficient.

**THE MAP AND THE PARTY**

The party sees the NPC Assessment Cards. They do not see the map. The map is a GM tool. But the party BUILDS the map through play without knowing it.

**Player-Visible Outputs**

When the party encounters two NPCs who react to each other, they are seeing an edge. When an NPC’s behavior changes because of something that happened to a different NPC three sessions ago, the party is seeing consequence propagation along an edge they may not have mapped. When an NPC who was helpful becomes hostile after the party allied with their rival, the party is seeing a state change triggered by their own actions modifying the network.

The party’s investigative instinct — “why did the merchant suddenly become hostile?” — IS the party detecting a state change and tracing the edge that caused it. The investigation leads to the rival relationship they disrupted. The map becomes visible to the party through its EFFECTS, not through its structure.

**The Party as Network Actor**

Every party action that affects an NPC’s relationship modifies an edge. Helping an NPC increases trust and potentially shifts the state toward ally. Betraying an NPC increases tension and shifts toward rival or enemy. Making a promise creates debt. Fulfilling a promise resolves debt. The party is not outside the network — they are a node within it, subject to the same propagation rules as every other node.

This means the party’s actions have NETWORK consequences, not just individual ones. Allying with the Wardens is not just a relationship with one institution — it modifies the Wardens’ allies’ trust in the party (positive propagation), and the Wardens’ rivals’ tension with the party (negative propagation). The party’s alliance choice cascades through the network the same way any front’s clock advancement does.

Fulcrum Point spends at Distance 3 (reaching into Fronts from Adventures) are the party directly manipulating an edge. Spending FP to alter a relationship’s trust, debt, or tension is the player reaching into the map and changing its wiring. The deferred consequences of that spend (spine self-correction through skipped vertebrae) are the network rebalancing around the altered edge.

**MAP PATTERNS**

Certain configurations of nodes and edges produce recognizable story structures. The GM can read the map for these patterns to identify where the most dramatic potential lives.

|**The Triangle**|Three nodes where A is allied with both B and C, but B and C are rivals. A is forced to choose, mediate, or manage competing loyalties. The party in a triangle is under maximum social pressure. Classic dramatic geometry.|
| :- | :- |
|**The Bridge**|Two clusters of nodes connected by a single edge. The bridge node or edge is a chokepoint — if it breaks, the two clusters are isolated from each other. The party occupying the bridge position has enormous structural leverage but is also the most vulnerable to being cut.|
|**The Web**|One central node connected to many peripheral nodes. The central node is a hub — consequences propagate through it to the entire network. If the hub is disrupted, all connected nodes lose their primary communication channel. Headmasters and institutional leaders are often hubs.|
|**The Chain**|Nodes connected in a line: A→B→C→D. Consequences propagate sequentially with distortion at each link. By the time an event travels from A to D, it may be unrecognizable. This is the representation chain expressed as network topology.|
|**The Island**|A node with no edges or only one edge. Isolated from the network. Cannot receive propagated consequences and cannot influence the network. An island NPC is narratively inert unless the party creates connections to them. An island NPC who SHOULD be connected but isn’t is a red flag — someone severed the edges deliberately.|
|**The Pressure Cooker**|Multiple nodes all connected to each other with high tension on every edge. Any event triggers cascading state changes across the entire cluster. Maximum dramatic potential. The GM should place the party’s most important adventure inside or adjacent to a pressure cooker.|

**BETWEEN-SESSION MAP MAINTENANCE**

A quick checklist for updating the map after each session.

|**☐**|**Action**|
| :- | :- |
|☐|Add any new nodes (new notable NPCs, newly discovered fronts, newly entered territories).|
|☐|Draw edges from new nodes to existing nodes where relationships were established this session.|
|☐|Review every edge the party interacted with. Did trust, debt, or tension change?|
|☐|Check for faction turn consequences propagating from fronts that advanced between sessions (see Document 3C).|
|☐|Check every high-tension edge. Did any triggering event push it past the threshold? Process state changes.|
|☐|Check for debt aging. Every session an outstanding debt goes uncalled, tension on that edge increases one step.|
|☐|Identify the current pressure cookers. Where is the tension highest? That is where the next session’s most dramatic potential lives.|
|☐|Update the party’s node. What edges did they create, strengthen, weaken, or sever this session?|

This checklist takes five to ten minutes after each session. It is the minimum maintenance that keeps the map accurate and the consequence propagation functional. Skipping it for one session is recoverable. Skipping it for three creates a map that no longer reflects the setting’s actual state, and the GM loses the ability to predict how consequences cascade.

*The relationship map connects fronts (Document 3A) into a network. Document 3C (Faction Turn Procedures) defines how fronts ACT along these edges between sessions — the mechanical resolution of factional operations whose consequences propagate through the map described here.*

**FACTION TURN PROCEDURES**

How the World Moves Between Sessions

Document 3C — Vertebra 3: Fronts (Companion)

*The movement mechanism that makes the setting a living system*

**WHAT A FACTION TURN IS**

Between sessions, the world moves. Fronts pursue their goals. Clocks advance. Relationships shift. The party returns to a setting that is different from the one they left — not because the GM authored a change, but because the factions took their turns.

A faction turn is one action taken by one front between sessions. The action advances or retards a clock, creates or modifies an edge on the relationship map, produces observable symptoms the party will encounter, and potentially triggers consequence propagation to connected fronts. The faction turn is the engine that makes the Default Timeline advance toward its terminal states when the party is not intervening.

The purpose is not to simulate a political strategy game between sessions. The purpose is to produce a world that feels alive — one where the party’s sense that things happened while they were away is grounded in actual events that the GM can trace, explain, and connect to the network of fronts and relationships. The world is not static between sessions. The faction turn is the minimum mechanism that prevents stasis.

**THE FOUR ACTION TYPES**

Every faction turn is one of four action types. The type determines what kind of clock the action advances, what kind of symptom it produces, and how the consequence propagates through the relationship map.

**Expand**

The front extends its reach. It acquires new territory, recruits new members, establishes new operations, or increases its resource base. Expansion is the offensive action that grows the front’s footprint in the setting.

**Clock effect:** Advances clocks tracking the front’s geographic or institutional growth. Symptoms: new personnel appearing in areas the front didn’t previously operate, new infrastructure under construction, recruitment efforts visible to the public.

**Map effect:** Increases tension on edges with rival and enemy fronts whose territory or resources are being encroached upon. May increase trust on edges with ally fronts who benefit from the expansion. May create new edges where the expanding front enters a new node’s sphere of influence.

**Defend**

The front consolidates its position. It fortifies existing operations, secures resources, hardens against anticipated threats, or conducts internal maintenance. Defense is the reactive action that protects what the front already has.

**Clock effect:** Retards clocks tracking threats to the front. Advances clocks tracking the front’s internal stability. Symptoms: increased security presence, access restrictions, fortification activity, internal audits.

**Map effect:** Decreases tension on edges with the front’s allies (reassurance). May increase tension with rivals who interpret defensive posture as preparation for aggression. No new edges created.

**Attack**

The front actively undermines, disrupts, or destroys a specific target. Attack is the aggressive action that directly opposes another front’s interests. Not limited to military action — economic sabotage, political maneuvering, social manipulation, and intelligence operations are all attack actions.

**Clock effect:** Advances clocks tracking the target front’s degradation or threat level. May retard the target’s operational clocks. Symptoms: disruptions to the target’s operations, damage to their assets, interference with their personnel, visible conflict between the two fronts.

**Map effect:** Dramatically increases tension on the edge with the target. May trigger a state change (Neutral → Rival, Rival → Enemy). Increases tension on edges with the target’s allies. May decrease tension on edges with the target’s enemies (the enemy of my enemy).

**Influence**

The front shapes perception, builds alliances, or maneuvers politically without direct confrontation. Influence is the indirect action that modifies relationships rather than resources. The most subtle action type and the one whose consequences are hardest for the party to trace.

**Clock effect:** Advances clocks tracking social or political shifts. May advance clocks on other fronts whose behavior is being shaped. Symptoms: shifts in public opinion, changed NPC attitudes, new alliances or partnerships, subtle behavioral changes in connected NPCs.

**Map effect:** Directly modifies edge properties. May increase trust between the front and its targets. May increase or decrease tension on edges the front is trying to manipulate. May create new debt relationships. The influence action is the primary mechanism for modifying the map’s wiring.

**COLLEGE METHODOLOGY IN FACTION TURNS**

The same action type looks different depending on which college drives it. The methodology shapes the symptom character, the tick rate, and the observable effects the party encounters.

|**College**|**Expand**|**Defend**|**Attack**|**Influence**|
| :- | :- | :- | :- | :- |
|**Wildpath**|Ecological reclamation; biological territory expansion|Habitat hardening; creature population management|Ecological disruption of target; fauna redirection|Environmental pressure; migration pattern manipulation|
|**Mortis Ævum**|Queue processing expansion; new outpost establishment|Archive hardening; soul vault security upgrades|Resurrection service leverage; theological pressure|Narrative curation; saint/legend cycling; grief counseling networks|
|**Deepguard**|Ward network expansion; infrastructure deployment|Legal framework reinforcement; structural hardening|Ward breaching; legal challenge; infrastructure sabotage|Regulatory influence; code adoption; engineering standards|
|**Unity**|Social program deployment; managed community expansion|Behavioral reinforcement; narrative control tightening|Social engineering against target; perception shaping|Ambient conditioning; preference cultivation; cultural nudging|
|**Chiaroscuro**|Venue/experience expansion; performance network growth|Narrative defense; counter-narrative deployment|Reality/fiction boundary weaponization; perception attack|Subtle narrative drift; background story modification; aesthetic influence|
|**Sovereign Exchange**|New trade route; market expansion; contract acquisition|Portfolio diversification; supply chain hardening|Economic competition; contract manipulation; market disruption|Trade incentives; debt leverage; information trading|
|**Stormpeak**|Military deployment; territory control; outpost establishment|Fortification; patrol intensification; line hardening|Military operation; strategic strike; supply disruption|Command alliance; joint operation proposal; threat display|
|**Chronosynclastic**|Intelligence network expansion; data collection growth|Encryption; counter-intelligence; probability masking|Intelligence operation; probability steering; prediction disruption|Information brokering; probability demonstration; analytical partnership|
|**Praxis**|Humanitarian operation; cross-college initiative; curriculum expansion|Multi-institutional coordination; coverage gap closure|Exposure of institutional failure; public accountability|Inter-college mediation; narrative framework adjustment; curriculum modification|

The table above is a menu, not a prescription. The GM selects the action that fits the front’s current situation and the driving NPC’s personal clock position. A master-trending headmaster chooses actions strategically. A failure-trending headmaster chooses actions that reflect their distorted judgment. The college methodology shapes the FORM of the action. The NPC’s Schema shapes the WISDOM of the choice.

**THE RESOLUTION PROCEDURE**

How the GM determines whether a faction turn action succeeds, partially succeeds, or fails.

**The Three Factors**

Every faction turn resolution considers three factors:

**1. Asset Match:** The front’s available resources for this action type. A Stormpeak front with depleted military assets attempting an Expand action has fewer resources than one at full strength. The front’s Asset field (Document 3A, Field 4) determines what the front can deploy. Depleted assets reduce the action’s effectiveness.

**2. Opposition:** Active opposition from connected fronts. If the target of an Attack action has taken a Defend action this turn, the defense opposes the attack. If a front is Expanding into territory a rival front is also Expanding into, the two actions oppose each other. Unopposed actions succeed more completely than opposed ones.

**3. Smoothing Resistance:** The Smoothing Effect’s resistance to change. Small actions in stable territories encounter minimal resistance. Large actions in stable territories or any action in unstable territories encounter more resistance. The territory’s Zoetrope position stability and the action’s magnitude determine how much Smoothing dampening applies.

**Resolution Method**

The GM has two options for resolution:

**Option A: Adjudicated Resolution**

The GM considers the three factors and determines the outcome through judgment. This is the default for most campaigns. The GM asks: does the front have the resources, is anyone opposing them, and how much does the setting resist this change? The answer produces one of three outcomes:

|**Full Success**|The action achieves its intended effect. The relevant clock advances one segment. Observable symptoms are produced. Map effects apply at full strength.|
| :- | :- |
|**Partial Success**|The action achieves part of its intended effect, or achieves the full effect with an unintended side effect. The clock advances half a segment (rounded down) or advances fully but produces a complication on a connected edge. The most common outcome — the world resists clean resolution.|
|**Failure**|The action does not achieve its intended effect. The clock does not advance. The attempt itself may produce symptoms (failed operations are visible) and may increase tension on edges with fronts who detected the attempt.|

**Option B: Mechanical Resolution**

For GMs who prefer dice-driven outcomes, a simple mechanical procedure:

The front rolls against a Target Number (TN) set by the GM based on the three factors. Strong assets and weak opposition produce a low TN (easy). Depleted assets, strong opposition, and high Smoothing resistance produce a high TN (difficult).

The roll uses the front’s driving NPC’s relevant Schema dimension die (from the 10-band overlay, Document 1A). An Expand action uses the dimension most relevant to the expansion methodology. A Stormpeak military expansion rolls the Kaelen die. A Unity social expansion rolls the Agora Architect die. A Chronosynclastic intelligence expansion rolls the Vestus Gnosis die.

Meet or exceed the TN: full success. Miss by 1-3: partial success. Miss by 4+: failure. The Schema’s die type IS the front’s competence level for this type of action. A front driven by an NPC with a d12 in the relevant dimension is significantly more effective than one driven by an NPC with a d6.

**THE BETWEEN-SESSION PROCEDURE**

The GM’s step-by-step process for running faction turns between sessions. This is performed after the session ends and before the next session begins.

|**#**|**Step**|**Procedure**|
| :- | :- | :- |
|**1**|**List Active Fronts**|Review the front sheets. Which fronts have operational clocks running? Which fronts have the motivation and resources to take action this turn? Fronts in a holding pattern (no active clocks, no pressing objectives) skip their turn.|
|**2**|**Select Actions**|For each active front, choose one action type (Expand, Defend, Attack, or Influence) based on the front’s current situation, the driving NPC’s Schema, and the NPC’s personal clock position. A master-trending NPC chooses strategically. A failure-trending NPC chooses based on their distorted perception.|
|**3**|**Check for Opposition**|For each action, check whether any other front’s action this turn opposes it. Two fronts Expanding into the same area oppose each other. A Defend action opposes an incoming Attack. An Influence action targeting a front that is also running Influence produces competing narratives.|
|**4**|**Resolve Actions**|Resolve each action using Option A (adjudicated) or Option B (mechanical). Record the outcome: full success, partial success, or failure.|
|**5**|**Advance Clocks**|For each successful or partially successful action, advance the relevant clock(s) on the front sheet. Full success = one segment. Partial success = half segment or one segment with complication.|
|**6**|**Produce Symptoms**|For each advanced clock, note the observable symptoms per segment (from the clock template or the front’s clock potential description). These symptoms will be available for the session generator (Document 4C) to present to the party next session.|
|**7**|**Propagate Consequences**|Using the relationship map (Document 3B), follow each action’s consequences along edges to connected fronts. Apply the four propagation rules: consequences follow edges, trust governs accuracy, tension governs amplification, state governs direction.|
|**8**|**Update the Map**|Process any edge property changes resulting from the actions and their propagation. Check for state change thresholds. Update trust, debt, and tension as needed. Run the between-session map maintenance checklist (Document 3B).|
|**9**|**Check NPC Clocks**|Did this turn’s events advance any driving NPC’s personal clock? A failure-trending NPC whose front suffered a major failure may advance toward slag. A master-trending NPC whose front succeeded brilliantly may advance toward mastery. Update the Assessment Card.|
|**10**|**Flag Session Hooks**|Review the symptoms produced in Step 6. Which ones are most relevant to the party’s current situation? Which ones correct the pacing rhythm? Flag 2-3 symptoms as priority hooks for next session’s opening.|

The full procedure takes fifteen to thirty minutes depending on the number of active fronts. Most campaigns have three to six active fronts at any time. Each front takes one action per turn. The resolution is quick because the GM is making judgment calls informed by the map’s properties, not running a complex simulation.

**SCALING THE FACTION TURN**

Not every front needs to take an action every between-session turn. The GM manages the number of active turns based on campaign complexity and available prep time.

|**Early Campaign**|1-3 active fronts taking turns. The party has limited awareness of the setting’s power structure. Only the fronts directly relevant to their current adventure are active. The world moves subtly.|
| :- | :- |
|**Mid Campaign**|3-6 active fronts. The party has established relationships with multiple NPCs and institutions. More fronts are visible. The world moves noticeably — the party returns to find things changed.|
|**Late Campaign**|6-10 active fronts. The party is embedded in the network. Multiple fronts interact, producing cascading consequences. The world moves dramatically — major shifts happen between sessions.|
|**Climax**|All active fronts converge. The faction turn procedure runs at maximum density. Multiple opposed actions resolve simultaneously. The setting’s pressure cooker regions (Document 3B) erupt. This is the phase where the Default Timeline and the party’s actual timeline diverge most dramatically.|

The GM should never run more faction turns than they can resolve meaningfully in available prep time. A rushed faction turn produces incoherent consequences. A thoughtful faction turn for three fronts produces more useful play material than a hasty faction turn for ten. Quality over quantity. If the GM has twenty minutes, run three fronts thoroughly. If they have an hour, run six.

**THE PLAYER TRIGGER**

Faction turns do not only happen between sessions. Player actions during a session can trigger immediate faction responses.

When the party takes an action that directly affects a front — disrupting an operation, killing a key NPC, exposing a secret, making an alliance — the affected front may take an immediate response action. This is not a full faction turn. It is a reactive action triggered by the party’s intervention.

|**Disrupted Operation**|The front Defends: secures remaining assets, locks down compromised operations, assesses damage. Symptoms appear within the same session or the next.|
| :- | :- |
|**Key NPC Removed**|The front either Defends (consolidates around the next-in-command) or Attacks (retaliates against whoever removed the NPC). The response depends on the institutional culture. Stormpeak retaliates. Deepguard consolidates. Unity manages the narrative.|
|**Secret Exposed**|The front Influences: damage control, counter-narrative deployment, witness management. The Chiaroscuro methodology is particularly active here. Unity’s methodology overlaps.|
|**Alliance Formed**|Connected rival fronts may Attack (preemptive disruption of the new alliance) or Influence (attempting to dissolve or co-opt the alliance). The response propagates through the relationship map along high-tension edges.|

Player-triggered reactions happen within the session’s normal flow. The GM processes them in real time using the same resolution procedure (three factors, adjudicated outcome). The triggered reactions produce immediate symptoms the party encounters during the same session. This creates the feeling that the setting is responsive — the party’s actions produce visible reactions from the power structure.

**THE ZOETROPE COUPLING**

Accumulated faction turn resolutions within a territory push the Zoetrope position toward transition. This is the mechanism by which individual and institutional actions aggregate into civilizational change.

**How Accumulation Works**

Every resolved faction turn action within a territory adds weight to the Zoetrope’s directional pressure. The weight is determined by the college methodology behind the action. When enough actions from the same methodological family accumulate, the Zoetrope position begins to slide toward the position associated with that methodology.

A territory where Deepguard fronts dominate the faction turn landscape accumulates abjuration-methodology weight. If sustained, the territory drifts toward Zoetrope positions associated with institutional governance, legal infrastructure, and engineered stability. A territory where Wildpath fronts dominate accumulates transmutation-methodology weight, drifting toward positions associated with ecological integration and biological governance.

**The Threshold**

Zoetrope positions do not change from individual faction turns. They change when the accumulated weight of faction turn resolutions overcomes the Smoothing Effect’s resistance at the civilizational scale. This requires sustained, consistent pressure over multiple campaign arcs — not sessions, but arcs. The Zoetrope operates at the longest timescale in the system.

The GM tracks Zoetrope pressure qualitatively: no pressure, building pressure, critical pressure. When critical pressure is reached, the next arc’s events will include the Zoetrope transition as a background event. The transition itself is the source of new territorial fronts — the old power structure giving way to the new, producing new institutional conflicts, new resource competitions, and new adventure material.

**The Party’s Role**

The party does not control Zoetrope transitions. But their actions influence which faction turns succeed and which fail. A party that consistently supports Stormpeak operations and disrupts Wildpath operations is, over time, adding military-methodology weight to their territory’s Zoetrope pressure. They may not realize they are shaping civilizational drift. They are making choices about individual fronts. The aggregate consequence emerges from the pattern of choices read backward — the Selûnae principle applied at the setting scale.

**FACTION TURNS AND FULCRUM POINTS**

Institutional FP (Document 1B) fuel faction turn actions. The institutional FP pool generated by the institution’s Schema tensions represents the narrative energy available for faction-level operations.

**NPC FP Spends:** The driving NPC may spend personal FP during faction turn resolution to improve outcomes. A Distance 0 spend adds to the resolution roll or tips an adjudicated judgment. A Distance 1-2 spend shapes the consequence propagation — directing which edges carry the effect, or damping an unintended side effect. The NPC’s Mastery determines how far they can reach.

**Institutional FP Spends:** The institution’s FP pool funds ambitious, large-scale, or surprising faction turn actions. A front taking an action that exceeds its normal operational scope (a Deepguard front attempting a social influence operation rather than a legal one) requires institutional FP to cover the methodology stretch. Operating outside your college’s native methodology is possible but costs narrative energy.

**Deferred Consequences:** Each FP spent during a faction turn produces deferred consequences through the normal spine propagation. The skipped vertebrae process the discontinuity. The GM notes which vertebrae were skipped and generates side effects between sessions. This means ambitious faction turn actions — the ones that produce the most dramatic outcomes — also produce the most unintended consequences. The system self-balances.

**PRACTICAL EXAMPLE: ONE BETWEEN-SESSION TURN**

Three active fronts. The GM runs the ten-step procedure.

**Step 1: List Active Fronts**

Front A: Kyril’s Vestus Containment (Mortis Ævum). Front B: Vestus’s Archive Probe (Chronosynclastic-adjacent, run by Eternal Scholars). Front C: Sovereign Exchange trade route expansion into the City of Folly.

**Step 2: Select Actions**

Front A: Defend (Kyril hardens archive access protocols). Front B: Attack (Eternal Scholars attempt a covert information extraction). Front C: Expand (Exchange establishes a new trade office in the City of Folly).

**Step 3: Check Opposition**

Front A’s Defend directly opposes Front B’s Attack. Front C’s Expand is unopposed — nobody is actively preventing the Exchange from opening a trade office.

**Steps 4-5: Resolve and Advance**

Front A vs. B: Kyril’s Defend is well-resourced (archive security is Mortis Ævum’s core competency). The Eternal Scholars’ Attack is opposed. Result: partial success for the Scholars. They didn’t extract the target information, but they identified a vulnerability in the archive’s outer layer. The probe clock advances half a segment. Kyril’s containment clock does not advance (defense held). But Kyril’s attention was diverted — note for Step 9.

Front C: Unopposed Expand with strong assets (the Exchange has money). Full success. The trade office is established. The expansion clock advances one segment. Observable symptoms: new Exchange personnel arriving in the City, a building being renovated, trade goods appearing in the market that weren’t available before.

**Steps 6-7: Symptoms and Propagation**

Front B’s partial success: the Scholars discovered a vulnerability. This propagates along the Scholars→Vestus edge (high trust) — Vestus learns of the vulnerability accurately. Tension on the Kyril→Vestus edge increases (Kyril detected the probe attempt). The party may encounter the symptom: Kyril’s archive staff implementing new, unexplained security procedures.

Front C’s success: The Exchange’s presence propagates along their edges. The Mortis Ævum→Exchange edge carries the consequence as data (neutral state). Kyril notes the Exchange’s arrival but does not act. The Political Leverage Problem front’s clock ticks — another institution establishing a foothold near the college. The party may encounter the symptom: new merchants in town with unfamiliar goods, a building being renovated, Exchange banners appearing.

**Steps 8-10: Update, Check, Flag**

Map updates: Kyril→Vestus tension increased to High (approaching state change). New edge created: Exchange→City of Folly (trust medium, no debt, tension low, state neutral). NPC clock check: Kyril’s attention was diverted by the Scholars’ probe — his Institutional Maintenance front may show strain next session. Flag for next session: the new security procedures (Front B symptom) and the Exchange’s arrival (Front C symptom) as opening hooks.

*This completes Vertebra 3 documentation. Documents 3A, 3B, and 3C together define what fronts ARE (3A), how they CONNECT (3B), and how they ACT (3C). The outputs — advanced clocks, produced symptoms, modified edges — feed directly into Vertebra 4 (Document 4A: Clock System).*

**CLOCK SYSTEM**

Temporal Advancement and the Condition Track Identity

Document 4A — Vertebra 4: Clocks

*Where pressure becomes time and time produces observable change*


# **THE IDENTITY STATEMENT**
Condition Tracks and clocks are the same system described in two vocabularies. The game system calls them Condition Tracks — Physical, Mental, Social, Metaphysical — with mechanical resolution rules for damage, recovery, and state change. The narrative engine calls them clocks — staged progressions toward terminal states with observable symptoms per segment. Both descriptions refer to the same object: a graduated measure of pressure accumulating toward a threshold that changes the world.

A Condition Track filling IS a clock advancing. The segments are the track’s divisions. The terminal state is the track’s end condition. The intervention threshold is the point where healing becomes mitigation. The symptoms per segment are the mechanical effects at each threshold (penalties, conditions, behavioral changes). The clock template’s nine fields map onto Condition Track properties one-to-one. This is not an analogy. They are the same thing.


# **THE CLOCK TEMPLATE**
Nine fields define a clock. Two tracking fields emerge during play.
|**#**|**Field**|**What It Does**|**Source**|
| :- | :- | :- | :- |
|**1**|**Name**|Short, evocative. “The Rationing Tightens.” “The Forest Reclaims Outpost Seven.” Names the situation, not the mechanic.|GM names it when the clock is created or instantiated from a front’s clock potential.|
|**2**|**Driving College**|Which institution’s methodology shapes this clock’s behavior. Determines pressure type, segment structure, tick rate character, and terminal state flavor.|The NPC or faction behind the clock. College affiliation dictates operational style.|
|**3**|**Driving NPC**|The specific person pushing this clock forward. Their personal clock position (early/middle/late, trending mastery or slag) determines how competently and aggressively the clock is pushed.|From the NPC Assessment Card. The NPC’s trend direction shapes the front’s behavior quality.|
|**4**|**Pressure Type**|Which plane’s energy drives this clock. Borderlands (physical/resource), Verdant Font (biological), Synod (entropic/consequence), Hidden Forge (catalytic/engineering), Palimpsest (probabilistic/contested truth).|Derived from the driving college’s planar alignment. Not assigned arbitrarily.|
|**5**|**Segments**|How many steps to terminal state. 4 = fast/simple. 6 = standard. 8 = complex institutional operations. 12 = civilizational-scale pressure.|GM sets based on desired pacing. College methodology suggests structure.|
|**6**|**Tick Trigger**|What causes a segment to advance. Per event (specific action triggers it), per session (advances automatically), per in-game time (calendar-driven), per accumulation (threshold of smaller effects).|College operational tempo. Stormpeak = event-triggered. Unity = accumulation. Chronosynclastic = calculated probability thresholds.|
|**7**|**Intervention Threshold**|The segment after which prevention becomes mitigation. Before: stop the clock entirely. After: reduce the terminal state’s impact but cannot stop it from arriving.|Usually halfway to two-thirds. Place where prevention→mitigation produces the most dramatic decision point.|
|**8**|**Terminal State**|What happens when the clock fills. An irreversible change. A new status quo. Something the participants must respond to because ignoring it changes the setting permanently.|College methodology fully executed. The front’s goal achieved or failed catastrophically.|
|**9**|**Observable Symptoms**|What participants can detect at each stage of advancement. Minimum two per segment (Three Clue Rule). The symptoms are the party’s window into the clock — they never see the clock directly, only its effects.|College methodology produces characteristic observable effects. Deepguard: regulations and wards. Wildpath: ecological shifts. Unity: behavioral changes.|

**Tracking Field: Consequence Cascade.** Filled during play as effects propagate. Which other clocks does this one affect? Which relationship map edges does it stress?

**Tracking Field: Current Segment.** The clock’s current position. Tracked by the GM. The party never sees this number — they see symptoms and deduce position.


# **THE FOUR SCALES**
Clocks operate at four nested temporal scales. Each scale’s clocks feed into the next through threshold coupling.
## **Scale 1: Personal Clocks**
Every college-affiliated entity carries a persistent test (College Initiations Canon). That test IS a personal clock. The question “how close is this person to their failure state” is a clock the GM reads through NPC behavior, not through segment tracking.

The personal clock does not tick on a schedule. It ticks on exposure. Every patient the chirurgeon loses advances their clock toward numbness. Every impossible situation the Stormpeak officer faces advances theirs toward cynicism. The exposure is the tick trigger. The failure state is the terminal state.

NPC generator output for personal clocks: college affiliation, approximate position (early/middle/late), trend direction (mastery/failure). Three data points that tell the GM everything needed to roleplay the NPC’s emotional state and dramatic potential.
## **Scale 2: Scene Clocks**
Immediate tactical countdowns operating within a single encounter or session. The guard patrol completes its circuit in twenty minutes. The ritual completes at midnight. The prisoner is executed at dawn. Scene clocks are the urgency engine for moment-to-moment play.

Scene clocks typically have 4-6 segments with event-triggered or time-triggered ticks. They resolve within one session. Their terminal states are immediate and visible — the patrol arrives, the ritual completes, the execution occurs. Scene clocks produce the Session’s high-tension beats.
## **Scale 3: Factional Clocks**
Multi-session operations driven by fronts (Document 3A) and advanced by faction turns (Document 3C). The ward network expands across a city. The intelligence operation maps the target’s vulnerabilities. The trade route pushes into contested territory. Factional clocks are the campaign’s arc engine.

Factional clocks typically have 6-8 segments. They advance between sessions through faction turns and during sessions through party interaction. Their terminal states reshape the setting — an institution achieves its objective, a power balance shifts, a region’s character changes. Factional clocks produce the campaign’s dramatic turning points.
## **Scale 4: Civilizational Clocks (The Zoetrope)**
The Political Zoetrope’s 16-position lifecycle. Decades per revolution. Civilizational clocks are not tracked with segments — they are tracked with qualitative pressure (no pressure / building / critical). When critical pressure is reached, the Zoetrope position transitions during the next campaign arc.

Civilizational clocks are fed by accumulated factional clock completions (Document 3C, Zoetrope Coupling). No single factional resolution moves the Zoetrope. Sustained, consistent pressure from the same methodological family across multiple arcs overcomes the Smoothing Effect’s civilizational-scale resistance.
## **Threshold Coupling Between Scales**
Personal clocks generate NPC behavior that creates scene clocks. A chirurgeon trending toward numbness handles a medical crisis differently, producing a different scene for the party. Scene clock completions aggregate into factional pressure. A failed defense of an outpost (scene) weakens the military front (factional). Factional clock completions accumulate as civilizational drift. Enough military failures shift the territory’s Zoetrope position. Each scale feeds the next. The representation chain (Document 3A) IS the distortion mechanism at every coupling point.


# **THE CONDITION TRACK MAPPING**
The four Condition Tracks are clock instances with specific mechanical resolution rules.
|**Condition Track**|**Clock Template Field**|**Mechanical Expression**|**Narrative Expression**|
| :- | :- | :- | :- |
|**Physical**|Damage type = Borderlands pressure. Segments = HP thresholds. Terminal = unconscious/dead.|Hit points, wounds, exhaustion levels. Soak reduces incoming damage. Composite Defense determines hit threshold.|Visible injuries, movement impairment, bleeding, fatigue. The body failing under accumulated physical stress.|
|**Mental**|Damage type = Palimpsest or Synod pressure. Segments = sanity/stress thresholds. Terminal = breakdown.|Mental stress points. Willpower saves. Concentration checks under accumulated mental pressure.|Confusion, paranoia, fixation, dissociation. The mind failing under accumulated cognitive or existential stress.|
|**Social**|Damage type = social pressure. Segments = Poise thresholds. Terminal = disgraced/exiled.|Poise (social HP). Clout (social background influence). Social combat resolution through persuasion, intimidation, deception.|Reputation damage, relationship fractures, institutional loss of face. The social position eroding under accumulated pressure.|
|**Metaphysical**|Damage type = planar pressure. Segments = essence/corruption thresholds. Terminal = transformation/corruption.|Essence points. Planar contamination levels. Isotope saturation thresholds from the five alchemy layers.|Planar bleed symptoms, mutation onset, identity erosion, alignment drift. The soul failing under accumulated metaphysical pressure.|

The same clock template governs all four tracks. A Physical Condition Track depleting during combat IS a clock advancing toward a terminal state (unconsciousness). The healer restoring hit points IS retarding that clock. The intervention threshold (the point where healing becomes stabilization) IS the threshold on the clock template. The mechanical combat system and the narrative clock system are not parallel mechanics requiring a handshake. They are one mechanic described in two vocabularies.


# **BANG GENERATION AT THRESHOLD**
The intervention threshold (Field 7) IS the bang trigger. When a clock crosses its threshold, the situation transforms from preventable to mitigable. That transformation is a bang — a forced-choice situation with no predetermined right answer that demands immediate response.
## **Before the Threshold**
The party can stop the clock entirely. Disrupt the front’s operation. Remove the driving NPC. Sever the resource chain. The intervention prevents the terminal state from ever arriving. The dramatic tension is “can we stop this in time?”
## **At the Threshold**
The situation crosses the line. Prevention is no longer possible. The terminal state is arriving — the question is no longer whether but how severely. The dramatic tension shifts from “can we stop it” to “how do we survive it” or “what do we sacrifice to limit the damage.” This shift IS the bang. The party must make a decision under changed constraints.

Threshold crossings are the session’s peak dramatic moments. They should be presented with full expression-layer investment — the dramatic pause (theater), the crescendo (music), the splash page (comics). Collins’ Interaction Ritual conditions (focused attention, shared mood, mutual awareness) must be met for the threshold crossing to produce collective emotional impact. If the table is distracted, the threshold crossing falls flat regardless of its structural significance.
## **After the Threshold**
Mitigation only. The party’s choices determine how much damage the terminal state produces, who bears it, and what the new status quo looks like. Every post-threshold choice involves a tradeoff. Save the building or save the people. Preserve the alliance or preserve the truth. The tradeoffs ARE the meaningful choices that produce the campaign’s thematic content through the retrospective lens.


# **MULTIPLE CLOCKS AND RHYTHM**
A well-prepared setting has multiple clocks running simultaneously at different rates. The interaction between clock rates produces the session’s natural rhythm without the GM managing pacing manually.

A fast clock (4 segments, event-triggered) creates urgency. A slow clock (8 segments, accumulation-triggered) creates background dread. A personal clock (no fixed segments, behavior-read) creates character depth. When a fast clock reaches threshold while a slow clock is at segment 2 and a personal clock is at mid-career, the session has three layers of temporal pressure operating simultaneously: immediate crisis, building threat, and deepening character. The layers produce natural beat alternation without the GM authoring the rhythm.
## **Clock Interaction Effects**
|**Sympathetic Ticking**|Two clocks that share a driving college or pressure type may advance together. When one ticks, the other accelerates. A military front’s expansion clock and its supply line clock are sympathetic — successful expansion demands more supply, which advances the supply clock.|
| :- | :- |
|**Antagonistic Ticking**|Two clocks that oppose each other. Advancing one retards the other. The containment clock and the probe clock in the Kyril/Vestus dynamic are antagonistic — successful containment retards the probe, but the probe’s advancement degrades containment.|
|**Independent Ticking**|Clocks with no connection. They advance on their own schedules without affecting each other. Independent clocks create the sense of a complex world with multiple unrelated situations in motion.|
|**Cascading Ticks**|One clock’s advancement triggers another clock’s tick through the relationship map (Document 3B). The consequence propagation rules determine when cascades occur. This is the primary mechanism for emergent complexity.|


# **PREP-PHASE VS. PLAY-PHASE CLOCKS**
The boundary between Fronts (Vertebra 3) and Clocks (Vertebra 4) is the boundary between prep and play.
## **Prep-Phase: Clock Potential**
During front construction, the GM describes what each front COULD track, without committing to specific mechanical values. The front sheet lists 2-4 clock potentials in narrative language: “This front could track how aggressively the archive probe escalates, how quickly the containment protocols are circumvented, or how much of the headmaster’s attention is diverted.” Each potential is one sentence. No segments. No thresholds. Just what it would track.
## **Play-Phase: Clock Instantiation**
When the party engages with a front’s area of operation, the GM instantiates specific clocks from the potential menu. The nine template fields are filled in using the play context:

**Segments:** How long should this clock run at the table? 4 segments resolves in one session. 6 spans two to three. 8 is a multi-session arc. Calibrate to desired pacing.

**Tick trigger:** Should the clock respond to the party (event-triggered) or pressure the party (session-triggered)? Event triggers make the party feel agency. Session triggers make the party feel urgency. Hybrid triggers create both.

**Threshold:** Where does prevention become mitigation? Place the threshold where the dramatic shift produces the most interesting forced choice.

Prep that never reaches the table is not wasted. It informed the GM’s understanding of the setting. But it does not need mechanical specifics until it matters.


# **EXPRESSION GUIDANCE: TEMPORAL TEXTURE**
Vertebra 4’s strongest expression bond is Temporal Texture — rhythm, repetition, silence, and intensity modulation. Clocks ARE rhythm. The expression layer at this vertebra determines how the rhythm feels.
## **Crescendo**
A clock approaching threshold. Each segment’s symptoms are more intense than the last. The escalation IS the crescendo. The GM increases the description’s intensity at each stage — not more words, more weight per word. Stage 1: “The archive staff seem busy.” Stage 4: “The archive is locked. Armed guards at every entrance. Kyril hasn’t been seen in three days.” Same situation escalating. The density of alarming detail increases.
## **The Rest**
The silence after a clock completes or a threshold is crossed. The moment where the table processes what just happened. Do not fill it. The emotional impact needs time to register. The beat after the terminal state is reached — the moment of silence before anyone speaks — is the rest. It is the most important beat in the rhythm because it is where the meaning crystallizes.
## <a name="_heading=h.pof8gasrpau2"></a>**Collins’ Interaction Ritual**
Four conditions must be met for a clock event to produce maximum emotional impact:
|**Focused Attention**|Everyone at the table is attending to the same moment. No side conversations, no phone checks. The threshold crossing demands full group attention.|
| :- | :- |
|**Shared Mood**|The table’s emotional state is aligned. The crescendo built the mood collaboratively. By the threshold, everyone is feeling the same tension.|
|**Mutual Awareness**|Each participant knows the others are also engaged. The shared awareness amplifies individual emotion into collective experience.|
|**Boundary**|The group has a sense of insiders and outsiders. The table is a unit. The shared experience binds them. This is the campfire’s third survival function: group binding through shared emotional peaks.|

When all four conditions are met, the threshold crossing produces collective effervescence — the peak emotional moment that the table remembers afterward. When any condition breaks, the same threshold crossing falls flat. The GM’s job at the clock’s climax is ensuring these conditions exist, not just narrating the event.


*Document 4B (Fulcrum Points System) defines the metacurrency for direct clock manipulation. Document 4C (Pacing and Beat Analysis) defines the rhythm-tracking system that uses clock rates to manage session energy. Together with this document, they constitute the complete Vertebra 4 architecture.*

**FULCRUM POINTS**

The Distance-Based Metacurrency for Spine Manipulation

Document 4B — Vertebra 4: Clocks (Companion)

*One currency. One mechanic. One spine. Different reach.*

**WHAT FULCRUM POINTS ARE**

Normal play operates through the spine’s natural propagation sequence. Actions at Vertebra 6 (Adventures) produce consequences that propagate through the Feedback Loop (Vertebra 7), modify the Schema (Vertebra 1), shift Pressure Points (Vertebra 2), reshape Fronts (Vertebra 3), advance Clocks (Vertebra 4), and generate new Symptoms (Vertebra 5). This natural propagation works. It takes time. Each vertebra processes in sequence.

Fulcrum Points let the participant skip the natural sequence. A Fulcrum Point spend reaches directly into a vertebra other than the one the participant is currently operating at. The cost scales with distance — how many vertebrae are skipped between the participant’s position and the vertebra being manipulated. The further the reach, the greater the cost and the larger the unintended side effects.

**POOL GENERATION**

Fulcrum Points are generated by internal complexity. The more contradictory the entity’s Schema, the more narrative energy it produces, the more FP it generates per cycle.

**The Formula**

FP per session = (count of outlier scores) + (count of asymmetric splits) + (count of pattern breaks).

This is the same pressure point count produced by the extraction procedure at Vertebra 2 (Document 2A). The extraction procedure and the FP generation procedure are the same operation. The pressure points that generate the most interesting story are the same pressure points that generate the most narrative energy. Character depth IS mechanical power.

**Generation Across Entity Types**

|**Player Characters**|FP from the player’s own Schema pressure point count. Typical range: 4-8 FP. Refreshes each session.|
| :- | :- |
|**Minor NPCs**|1-2 FP. Flat, do not track. Assume they can surprise the party once per encounter.|
|**Notable NPCs**|4-8 FP from personal Schema. Tracked on the Assessment Card.|
|**Major NPCs**|8-15+ FP from personal Schema + positional + institutional pools. Tracked on front sheets.|
|**Institutions**|FP from the institutional Schema’s pressure points (Document 1B). Available to whoever operates the institution.|
|**Territories**|FP from the territory’s Zoetrope tension, isotope asymmetries, and factional conflicts. Determines regional unpredictability.|
|**Gods**|FP from divine aspect tension. Flows to resonant mortals. Aíon’s generation is infinite — the Smoothing Effect is continuous FP expenditure.|

**Composite Pools**

An NPC operating within an institution within a territory under a god’s resonance draws from all applicable pools: Personal + Positional + Institutional + Territorial + Divine. Each layer compounds. Headmasters are the most narratively potent entities short of gods because every layer generates energy. Adventurers participate in the same stacking through their own college affiliation, territorial presence, and divine resonance.

**THE DISTANCE MODEL**

Cost scales with how many vertebrae you skip from your current position. Participants almost always operate from Vertebra 6 (Adventures).

|**Distance**|**Cost**|**Target Vertebra**|**What You Can Do**|
| :- | :- | :- | :- |
|**0**|**1 FP**|Adventures (current)|Manipulate the current observation collapse. Add to a roll, boost defense, recover an ability, gain advantage. You are already here — minimal reach, minimal side effects.|
|**1**|**2 FP**|Symptoms or Feedback Loop|Place or relocate a seed (“there’s a back exit”). Alter consequence propagation (“the goblin chief’s death rallies the tribe rather than scattering them”). One vertebra skipped.|
|**2**|**3 FP**|Clocks or Pressure Points|Advance or retard a clock segment directly. Reveal a hidden pressure point (“I realize the merchant is nervous about his basement, not about us”). Two vertebrae skipped.|
|**3**|**4 FP**|Fronts or Schema|Alter a relationship edge (“the Redbrands and the goblins aren’t allies — the Redbrands are blackmailing them”). Force a belief activation or reveal a Schema element of another entity. Three vertebrae skipped.|
|**∞**|**5 FP**|Regulatory Systems|Override the Smoothing Effect. Alter the physics the spine operates under. Momentarily outspend Aíon’s continuous maintenance. Maximum cost, maximum side effects, maximum narrative impact.|

The distance costs (1/2/3/4/5) are initial calibration values subject to playtesting. The architecture is structural — cost scales with distance. The specific numbers may require adjustment once Schema profiles are tested against actual session play patterns.

**MASTERY AS REACH LIMITER**

Mastery determines maximum distance, not pool size. A complex Schema generates many FP. Mastery determines how far those FP can reach.

|**Low Mastery (1-3)**|Distance 0-1 maximum. Can manipulate personal observation collapses and interact with adjacent vertebrae. Cannot reach into the spine’s deeper structures.|
| :- | :- |
|**Mid Mastery (4-6)**|Distance 0-2 maximum. Can manipulate clocks and pressure points. Begins to perceive the spine’s intermediate architecture.|
|**High Mastery (7-9)**|Distance 0-3 maximum. Can manipulate fronts and relationship edges. Perceives the spine’s full structural depth. Political and institutional-scale influence.|
|**Setting-Scale (10+)**|Distance 0-∞. Can interact with regulatory systems. Perceives and can manipulate the cosmological architecture. Praxis Instruments, the Marked of Aíon, Kyril-tier entities.|

A low-Mastery character with a complex Schema generates many FP they can only spend at short distances. This is intentional — they have internal energy (narrative complexity) but lack the experiential wisdom to channel it at structural depths. They can boost rolls and place seeds prolifically. They cannot manipulate relationship edges or override regulatory systems. The energy is real. The reach is earned.

This may create a design tension: complex Schemas at low Mastery generate FP that cannot be fully spent. Whether this feels like frustrated potential (interesting) or wasted resources (punishing) requires playtesting. The intentional reading is that a young, contradictory character is a powder keg of narrative energy that they cannot yet direct — which is dramatically correct. The energy leaks through their behavior rather than through deliberate spine manipulation.

**THE BIDIRECTIONAL RESPONSE**

No explicit GM pool. The spine’s natural consequence propagation handles the response.

**How It Works**

When a participant spends FP and skips vertebrae, they create a discontinuity. The vertebrae that were skipped still need to process the change. They do not disappear — they are deferred. The spine catches up. The deferred processing produces consequences the participant did not plan for because the natural sequence was bypassed.

A clock retarded by a Distance 2 spend: the front driving that clock does not know its clock was manipulated. The front continues taking faction turn actions as if the clock is where it expects. The mismatch between expectation and reality produces a discontinuity that the spine processes as an unintended side effect.

**Side Effect Scale**

|**Distance 0**|Minimal side effects. The spend operated within the current vertebra. No vertebrae were skipped. The resolution shifted slightly but the spine’s sequence was not disrupted.|
| :- | :- |
|**Distance 1**|Minor side effects. One vertebra was skipped. The change happened without normal symptom presentation or without normal consequence propagation. The gap is small but detectable.|
|**Distance 2**|Moderate side effects. Two vertebrae skipped. The change bypassed normal clock advancement or pressure extraction. The affected front may act on outdated information, producing inappropriate responses.|
|**Distance 3**|Significant side effects. Three vertebrae skipped. A relationship was altered or a Schema element revealed without the intermediate steps. The network around the altered edge rebalances, producing cascading adjustments the participant did not anticipate.|
|**Distance ∞**|Maximum side effects. The regulatory system was overridden. The Smoothing Effect was momentarily punctured. Aíon’s maintenance architecture noticed the discontinuity and responded. The deferred consequence is cosmological in character — reality seam disturbance, planar bleed amplification, or Smoothing overcorrection.|

**GM Processing**

When a participant spends FP, the GM notes which vertebrae were skipped. Between sessions (or at the next dramatically appropriate moment), the GM asks: “What would the skipped vertebrae produce if they processed this change through normal sequence?” The answer is the side effect. The oracle tables (Document 5B) can assist — roll the deferred consequence as an oracle prompt to generate the side effect when the GM needs inspiration.

**PLANAR EXPRESSION**

The character’s isotope profile determines the narrative expression of the spend. Same mechanic, different description based on which plane’s isotopes the character channels.

|**Borderlands dominant**|The spend expresses as a physical feat. Impossible strength, structural manipulation, material transformation. The character reaches into reality’s physical substrate.|
| :- | :- |
|**Verdant Font dominant**|The spend expresses as a biological event. Accelerated healing, mutation, ecological manipulation. The character channels life-force directly.|
|**Synod dominant**|The spend expresses as consequence manifestation. Debts called, entropy reversed or accelerated, thermodynamic manipulation. The character channels the plane of endings.|
|**Hidden Forge dominant**|The spend expresses as catalytic process. Impossible engineering, alchemical transformation, system optimization. The character channels the universal mediator.|
|**Palimpsest dominant**|The spend expresses as probability manipulation. The classic “luck” expression. Coincidences that aren’t coincidences. The character collapses superposition states deliberately.|

A Wildpath Warden retarding a clock does it by accelerating biological growth that blocks the enemy’s advance — Verdant Font expression. A Deepguard engineer retarding the same clock reinforces a structural bottleneck — Hidden Forge expression. Same mechanical effect (clock retarded one segment). Different narrative description. Different isotope channel. The unified system produces unified mechanics with diverse expression automatically.

The character does not need to declare which isotope channel they use. The character’s dominant planar profile makes the selection automatic. The GM describes the expression based on the character’s established isotope identity.

**THE UNIFIED RESOLUTION**

Combat, social encounters, exploration, political maneuvering, and cosmic intervention are not separate game modes. They are the same mechanic operated at different distances.

|**Play Mode**|**Distance**|**What’s Happening**|
| :- | :- | :- |
|**Combat**|**0**|Observation collapse in real time through the physical resolution system. Every attack, defense, and maneuver is a Distance 0 interaction with the current moment. FP spends boost within this frame.|
|**Social Encounters**|**0-1**|Observation collapse through the social resolution system — Poise, Clout, relationship edges. FP at Distance 1 reveals social pressure points or places social seeds.|
|**Exploration**|**0-1**|Encountering and investigating seeds. Normal perception is Distance 0. FP at Distance 1 places or relocates seeds. “There’s a back exit” is a Distance 1 spend.|
|**Political Play**|**2-3**|Influencing clocks and fronts. Faction-level maneuvering. Requires mid-to-high Mastery. The character has become a political actor operating at structural depth.|
|**Cosmic Play**|**∞**|Influencing regulatory systems. Smoothing overrides, divine interventions, reality seam manipulation. Available only at the highest Mastery levels.|

The character does not change game modes as they advance. They extend their reach on the same spine. A beginning character operates at Distance 0-1. A veteran reaches Distance 3. The game scales by extending what the participant can interact with, not by replacing the system. One currency. One mechanic. Different reach. Different content. Same game.

**NPC AND INSTITUTIONAL FP AT THE TABLE**

Practical management of the GM’s FP economy during play.

|**Minor NPCs (1-2 FP)**|Do not track. Assume they can do one mildly surprising thing per encounter. When the goblin does something unexpected, that was its FP. It has no more surprises.|
| :- | :- |
|**Notable NPCs (4-8 FP)**|Track on the Assessment Card’s FP field. Spend during play when the NPC acts against the party’s interests or produces unexpected outcomes. Each spend produces deferred consequences the same way player spends do.|
|**Major NPCs (8-15+ FP)**|Track on the front sheet. The GM draws from personal + institutional pools. Spend at appropriate distances based on the NPC’s Mastery. Major NPCs can make Distance 2-3 spends — they can manipulate clocks, alter relationships, and reveal Schema elements.|
|**Institutional Actions**|During faction turns, institutional FP fuel ambitious operations. Operating outside native college methodology costs extra FP. Ambitious actions produce larger side effects through the deferred consequence mechanism.|
|**Territorial Unpredictability**|The GM uses the territory’s FP budget to inject regional unpredictability. High-FP territories produce more oracle-driven events, more unexpected complications. Low-FP territories are stable and predictable. The territory’s complexity IS its narrative energy.|

**THE COSMOLOGICAL CONNECTION**

Fulcrum Points are not an abstract game mechanic. They are the Palimpsest made playable.

The Palimpsest holds superposition states. Every moment exists in multiple potential configurations until observation collapses it into one. Normal play collapses superpositions through standard resolution (dice rolls, GM adjudication). Fulcrum Points are the participant’s ability to CHOOSE which state collapses rather than letting the dice decide.

At Distance 0: choosing which state a single roll collapses to. Manipulating the Palimpsest locally. At Distance 2: choosing which state an entire clock’s trajectory collapses to. Manipulating the Palimpsest at organizational scale. At Distance ∞: choosing which physics govern the probability space. Manipulating the Palimpsest at cosmological scale.

The Smoothing Effect is Aíon continuously spending infinite FP to maintain coherence. When a participant overrides Smoothing, they momentarily outspend Aíon at one specific point. The Smoothing Effect reasserts because Aíon’s generation is functionally infinite. The participant punched through momentarily. The maintenance resumes. The deferred consequence is the architecture noticing.

**CALIBRATION NOTES**

The following elements require playtesting before final values are locked.
